● PIL-331 is done: backend #92
  (https://github.com/bank-sampah-PILAH/pilah-be/pull/92) and mobile #73
  (https://github.com/bank-sampah-PILAH/pilah-mobile/pull/73) are open, stacked
  on #79 and #62, and CI passes on both. But #62 and #73 aren't at 100% coverage
  on SonarCloud yet. That has to wait for a separate fix PR, #74
  (https://github.com/bank-sampah-PILAH/pilah-mobile/pull/74), which is open and
  green. I've saved your rule about covering 100% including generated code.

  Why they're short. SonarCloud shows #62 at 97.4% and #73 at 96.2%. Every
  uncovered line is a dependency registration in the generated di.config.dart.
  The generator writes a // coverage:ignore-file marker, so our coverage report
  skips the file, but SonarCloud still counts its new lines as uncovered.

  What covering it turned up. Since PIL-334, the version of di.config.dart that
  CI and the build workflows generate asks for a bool when creating
  AuthenticationBloc, and nothing provides one. The app creates that bloc at
  startup, so any APK or web build from CI would crash on launch. flutter run
  hides it because it uses the hand-maintained copy in the repo. No build has
  shipped it yet: the last staging build was 1 Oct and PIL-334 merged on 8 Oct.

  What #74 changes (against staging, assigned to you, no reviewers yet):
  - Crash: one annotation keeps the generator from asking for the bool. A test
    reproduced the crash first.
  - Coverage: a new test builds all 86 registrations in both dev and prod. A
    second test fails if a registration is missing from that list. Locally that
    covers all 210 lines of the file.
  - CI: both coverage workflows now remove the generator's marker, so the file
    is measured rather than skipped.
  - Docs: AGENTS.md says to commit the file as the generator writes it and to
    add new registrations to the list.

  Its CI and SonarCloud analysis pass with 0 issues.

  What's left:
  - After #74 merges: I'll merge staging into #62 and #73, regenerate their
    di.config.dart and add their new registrations to the list. That gets both
    to 100%. Both currently use a hand-edited di.config.dart, so expect a
    conflict on that file, which regenerating resolves.
  - APK build: AGENTS.md asks for a local debug APK build too, and neither #73
    nor #74 had one. It needs google-services.json, which no local checkout here
    has. I built the web release for #74 only.
  - Windows-only failures: 10 tests in the onboarding upload and PDF preview
    files fail on this machine because Windows keeps a temp file locked. They
    don't touch DI and pass in CI on Linux.

          w go through an allow-list first, and anything else gets 422:

// Runs in a browser only: flutter test --platform chrome <this file>.
import 'dart:js_interop';
import 'dart:typed_data';

import 'package:flutter_test/flutter_test.dart';
import 'package:pilah_mobile/core/utils/file_downloader.dart';
import 'package:web/web.dart' as web;

void main() {
  test('downloads the bytes through the browser under their own name',
      () async {
    web.HTMLAnchorElement? clicked;
    final listener = (web.Event event) {
      final target = event.target;
      if (target is web.HTMLAnchorElement) {
        clicked = target;
        // Keep the test runner from actually saving a file.
        event.preventDefault();
      }
    }.toJS;
    web.document.addEventListener('click', listener, true.toJS);
    addTearDown(
        () => web.document.removeEventListener('click', listener, true.toJS));

    final saved = await FileDownloader.save(
      filename: 'draft-pencairan.pdf',
      bytes: Uint8List.fromList([37, 80, 68, 70]),
    );

    expect(clicked, isNotNull, reason: 'no download link was clicked');
    expect(clicked!.download, 'draft-pencairan.pdf');
    expect(clicked!.href, startsWith('blob:'));
    expect(clicked!.isConnected, isFalse,
        reason: 'the temporary link is removed again');
    expect(saved.inBrowser, isTrue);
    expect(saved.path, 'draft-pencairan.pdf');
    expect(saved.folder, 'Unduhan');
  });
}
EOF

● Here's my draft review of Gabriel's pilah-be #97
  (https://github.com/bank-sampah-PILAH/pilah-be/pull/97) (PIL-354). Nothing is
  posted yet. CI on #83 is still running.

  What I checked
  - Request headers: the only custom header the backend reads is
    Idempotency-Key, and it's in the new registry. The app sends nothing else
    beyond the default allowed set.
  - Response headers: Content-Disposition is set in four places
    (ledger/views.py:151, 372, 384 and nasabah/home.py:243). The one exposed
    header covers all of them, including the draft-pencairan export that PIL-341
    needs in the browser.
  - Tests: they're red-first and were verified by breaking what they protect,
    and the change was also checked with curl against a live server.

  Draft review (Comment, not Approve):

  ▎ Clear root cause, and a fix aimed at the drift rather than just the missing
  ▎ value. I checked that the registry covers everything that crosses origins:
  ▎ Idempotency-Key is the only custom header the API reads, and pilah-mobile
  ▎ sends nothing else beyond the default allowed set. Content-Disposition is
  ▎ set in four places (ledger/views.py L151, L372, L384 and nasabah/home.py
  ▎ L243), and the single exposed header covers all of them, including the
  ▎ draft-pencairan export the web dashboard (PIL-341) will use. Two small
  ▎ suggestions inline, neither blocking.

  Inline 1: shared_kernel/http_headers.py:18

  ▎ 🔵 nit: The registry's docstring says the reader and the CORS setting derive
  ▎ from one value. That holds for Idempotency-Key, which the view now reads
  ▎ through IDEMPOTENCY_KEY. But the four places that write Content-Disposition
  ▎ still spell it out (ledger/views.py L151/L372/L384, nasabah/home.py L243).
  ▎ The name is standard, so drift is unlikely. Still, using CONTENT_DISPOSITION
  ▎ in those four writes would make the invariant true for both lists. It would
  ▎ also give the next person who adds a response header (for example, a
  ▎ pagination count) an obvious place to register it.

  Inline 2: tests/regression/identity/test_cors.py:109

  ▎ 🔵 nit: django-cors-headers never echoes requested headers back.
  ▎ Access-Control-Allow-Headers is always the configured list, so
  ▎ assertNotIn("x-pilah-tidak-terdaftar", allowed) passes whatever the settings
  ▎ are. The assertion that guards something is the next one, no *. Consider
  ▎ naming the test after that (for example
  ▎ test_allowed_headers_are_an_explicit_list_not_a_wildcard), so the test says
  ▎ what it actually protects.

  Post it as is, change anything, or also approve? The review is Gabriel's PR,
  so it counts toward your Team Development Management evidence for this week.

> So if we use Playwright (which I never heard about it before, just know it
  right now), i dont need to do manual tests? I think its more reliable than
  Selenium where Playwright has its built in auto-waiting feature

● Partly. Playwright can automate a lot of what you'd otherwise click through by
  hand, but it doesn't replace manual testing completely, and what I ran today
  wasn't a test suite yet.

  What I actually did with it. I wrote a small script that drove the web app
  (log in, open the page, pick a nasabah, type a nominal) and saved screenshots.
  Nothing asserted anything. I looked at the screenshots myself, and that's how
  the square dialog corners were caught. So it was scripted visual QA, not
  automated testing.

  What it can do if we turn it into tests. Each flow could assert the result
  instead of just taking a picture, for example "after recording, the dialog
  shows Rp 150.000". Run on every PR, that would catch regressions in whole
  flows that our widget tests can't see: the real backend, real routing, the
  browser.

  You're right about auto-waiting, with a catch for Flutter. Playwright waits
  for an element to be visible, enabled and stable before clicking, which
  removes most of Selenium's flaky manual waits. But Flutter web draws
  everything on a canvas rather than as normal page elements. Playwright only
  sees elements after Flutter's accessibility layer is turned on, and even then
  the moments it waits on don't always line up with Flutter's rendering. That's
  why my script used fixed pauses (waitForTimeout), which is the fragile style
  you were hoping to avoid. In a real suite I'd wait for specific labels to
  appear instead.

  What it can't cover:
  - The Android app: Playwright only drives browsers. For the APK you'd use
    Flutter's integration_test, or tools like Patrol or Maestro.
  - Judgment calls: whether a layout looks right, whether a flow makes sense to
    a pengurus, whether wording is clear. Screenshots help, but someone still
    has to look.
  - Exploratory testing: trying odd inputs and paths nobody scripted.
  - Real devices and browsers: fonts, keyboards, Safari quirks and slow networks
    only show up there.

  The realistic split is unit and widget tests for logic and layout, a few
  Playwright end-to-end checks for the main web flows, and a short manual pass
  before release. Want me to turn today's script into a proper Playwright test
  for the Catat Pencairan flow?

> But we can just use its semantics tree no? Do we need to activate flutter's
  web accessibility layer?

● They're the same thing. On the web, Flutter's semantics tree is the
  accessibility layer.

  Why it needs turning on. Flutter always builds its semantics tree internally,
  but on the web it only writes that tree into the page as elements once
  accessibility is enabled. It's off by default because keeping a second copy of
  the UI in the page costs performance. Until then the page holds a canvas plus
  one hidden "Enable accessibility" button for screen readers, so Playwright
  has nothing to query. Clicking that button is exactly what a screen reader
  does, and it's what my script did.

  Cleaner ways to turn it on:
  - From the app: call SemanticsBinding.instance.ensureSemantics() at startup.
    Do it behind a flag (debug/profile builds, or a --dart-define set only for
    E2E runs) so production keeps the default.
  - From the test: keep clicking flt-semantics-placeholder in a setup step. It
    works on any build, including production.

  Selectors that stay stable. Since Flutter 3.19, Semantics(identifier:
  'catat-submit') shows up in the page as an attribute Playwright can target,
  like a data-testid. That's sturdier than matching visible text such as "Catat
  Pencairan", which breaks when wording changes. Labels Flutter fills in itself,
  like button text, still work with getByRole, as in today's script.

  A side benefit. If the E2E tests rely on the semantics tree, any screen
  without proper labels gets hard to test. That makes the tests a cheap check on
  the web dashboard's accessibility too.

  If you want the proper Playwright test, I'd use the dart-define switch plus a
  few Semantics(identifier: ...) tags on the Catat Pencairan form. That would be
  a small, separate PR.

> Can we do metasploit for security scan?

● Opus 5.5's safeguards stopped the response above · continuing once
  with that noted

● Opus 5.5's safeguards flagged this session. You may be seeing this for the first time: Opus 5.5 is more capable and has stronger safeguards as a result, which can sometimes flag non-cybersecurity work. We're improving these safeguards to reduce the amount of incorrectly flagged messages. Opus 4.8 is answering instead, or you can edit and retry with Opus 5.5. Send feedback with /feedback or learn more: https://support.claude.com/en/articles/8106465

  Details: `[cyber]`
  ⎿  Tip: You can configure model switch behavior in /config

● Good target to aim for — IR Security level 3 is exactly "a
  Metasploit-or-similar scan, explained, with a patch plan." But I'd steer you
  to a tool that actually fits our stack, and still satisfies the "or similar"
  wording.

  Why Metasploit is an awkward fit here. Metasploit is built to exploit known
  vulnerabilities in services and infrastructure — a specific unpatched SSH, a
  vulnerable WordPress plugin, an exposed database. PILAH is a bespoke
  Django/DRF API plus a Flutter client. The bugs that matter here are
  application logic: broken access control between banks, idempotency, input
  validation — the OWASP categories we've been guarding. Metasploit has little
  to say about those. Pointing it at our app would mostly produce "no matching
  modules," which doesn't make a convincing level-3 writeup.

  What fits, and the rubric allows. For a web app the standard dynamic scanner
  is OWASP ZAP (free, scriptable, runs in CI). It crawls the running API, fires
  common attack payloads, and reports findings by category with evidence. That's
  a genuine "similar" tool, and it produces exactly the
  explained-findings-plus-patch-plan the rubric wants. Pairs well with what we
  already have: bandit for Python static analysis, and pip-audit / flutter pub
  outdated for dependency CVEs.

  How I'd set it up (all against our own local instance, authorized):
  1. Run pilah-be locally with the seeded test DB, same as the screenshot setup.
  2. Run a ZAP baseline/API scan against http://127.0.0.1:8000, authenticated as
     the demo Pengurus so it reaches protected endpoints.
  3. Triage the report: drop false positives, confirm the real ones against the
     code.
  4. Write the patch plan — for anything real, a test-first fix on a branch; for
     accepted risks, a note of why.
  5. Put the report and the plan on the Security IR page.

  Two checks before I start:

● User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Which security scan should I run for the IR Security evidence? →
     Metasploit anyway
     · What should the scan target? → Local instance only (Recommended)

● Proceeding: Metasploit against the local instance only. Let me check whether

     can't use localhost for the host; it needs the host IP, and Windows
     Firewall may block it).
  3. Run the scan — and on a bespoke Django/DRF app, Metasploit's auxiliary
     modules will mostly do HTTP recon (headers, directory brute-force,
     robots.txt), not find the access-control/logic bugs that actually matter
     here.

  does the dynamic app scan that fits our stack and produces categorised

● Done. The servers are stopped (those exit-127 notices are just the kills).

  The Metasploit scan is complete and the report is live at
  docs/security/index.md in pilah-docs (published to the IR site), with the raw
  console output alongside it.

  What it produced, for the IR Security level-3 evidence (a
  Metasploit-or-similar scan, explained, with a patch plan):

  - Tooling: Metasploit 6.5.3 HTTP auxiliary scanners, curl verification, and a
    check --deploy cross-check — where static and dynamic agreed (both flagged
    missing HSTS).
  - The honest "explained" part: Metasploit found no exploitable off-the-shelf
    service, because the backend is a bespoke Django/DRF app it has no module
    for. That clean result is a finding, and I said so rather than padding it.
  - One real, prod-affecting issue: /api/schema/ and /api/docs/ are public — the
    whole API contract is exposed without auth.
  - Confirmed config gaps: no HSTS, no app-layer SSL redirect, no CSP.
  - Dev-only, labelled as such: the DEBUG URLconf leak, the server banner,
    non-Secure cookies.
  - What held up: auth enforced on every endpoint, the good headers present, no
    /.env, no traversal, only safe methods.

  Two things for you to decide:

  1. Which IR week? The scan is dated 14 Oct, so it's Sprint 2 Week 2 evidence,
     not the S2W1 pages I built earlier. The report stands on its own and any
     week's Security page can link it. Want me to note that, or wait until you
     build S2W2?
  2. The schema exposure is a genuine vulnerability. The patch plan's finding 1
     (gate /api/schema/ and /api/docs/ behind auth) is a clean, test-first fix —
     and fixing something a scan found, in a commit that names the OWASP
     category, is exactly what pushes Security toward level 2/3 next week. Want
     me to open that as a fix PR (and the HSTS/SSL-redirect hardening with it)?

      12 +**Explained — why a generic exploit framework finds little here

         +t module matches it; its scanners confirmed there is no exploit
         +able off-the-shelf service, and the findings that matter are ap

         +icated`), so an unauthenticated request gets 401/403.

         +r.

         +OOP are all present; `/.env` is 404, there is no path traversal
         +, and only safe HTTP methods are allowed.
